Aviation English

An aviation English curriculum for safety culture, operations control, maintenance coordination, irregular operations, ground handling, compliance, passenger escalation, and incident reporting.

For aviation operations managers, airline staff, maintenance coordinators, safety teams, dispatchers, airport operations staff, ground handlers, and aviation-adjacent professionals.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Use a direct stop-work message, describe an observation, and preserve the authorized restart boundary.
  • Give a timestamped operations summary that preserves separate aircraft and crew statuses.
  • Request the correct maintenance references and distinguish a proposed deferral from an authorized decision.
  • Correct conflicting information, separate update and departure times, and offer a verified assistance route.
  • Confirm task ownership and report a catering balance without confusing arrival, receipt, and completion.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All cases and local processes are fictional. This is a language book, not flight, dispatch, maintenance, security, or emergency training. It does not teach operational radio phraseology or authorize aircraft release. Follow current approved manuals, qualified personnel, and applicable authority requirements.

01Safety Culture and Stop-Work AuthorityA concern must remain visible under schedule pressure+

Your goal

Use a direct stop-work message, describe an observation, and preserve the authorized restart boundary.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

A concern must remain visible under schedule pressure

At 08:40 local time, ramp agent Dana observes an unidentified wet patch beside ground equipment at Stand 6. Dana invokes the fictional site's local stop-work process for the affected task and informs supervisor Malik. The equipment team has not assessed the patch or its source. Under this local process, the affected task stays stopped until the designated supervisor completes the required assessment and authorizes restart. Dana is not authorized to handle the substance or repair equipment. A colleague suggests continuing because the turnaround is behind schedule. No injury or equipment fault has been established.

Dana: Ramp agent / Malik: Ground-operations supervisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What did Dana directly observe?

Answer and explanation

D. An unidentified wet patch beside equipment

The brief establishes a visible condition but not its substance, source, or cause.

2. What is the affected task's current status?

Answer and explanation

B. Stopped under the local process

The local stop-work process remains active until the required assessment and authorization.

3. What does schedule pressure change about restart authority?

Answer and explanation

B. Nothing; the required authorization is still needed.

A deadline does not replace the fictional site's assessment and restart requirements.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Dana can report a wet patch without naming the substance or declaring an equipment failure. The observation warrants the local process, but it does not establish cause, injury, or the correct technical remedy.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
ramp
The airport area used for aircraft servicing and related ground activity.report a ramp concern
stand
A designated aircraft parking position.identify the affected stand
ground support equipment (GSE)
Equipment used to support aircraft ground operations.identify ground support equipment
hazard
A condition or object with potential to cause harm.report a potential hazard
safety risk
The assessed likelihood and severity of harm from a hazard.assess safety risk
stop-work authority
Authority under the relevant process to halt an unsafe or uncertain task.invoke stop-work authority
affected task
The specific activity subject to the concern or restriction.identify the affected task
escalation route
The defined path for raising a concern to the responsible people.follow the escalation route
restart authorization
Permission to resume after the required conditions are satisfied.obtain restart authorization
safety management system (SMS)
An organized approach to managing safety risks and controls.support the safety management system
hazard report
A record communicating a potentially harmful condition.submit a hazard report
risk control
A measure intended to reduce or manage risk.verify a risk control
safety assurance
Checking whether safety arrangements remain effective.support safety assurance
safety promotion
Training and communication that support safe working practices.strengthen safety promotion
just culture
An approach balancing fair reporting, learning, and accountability.support a just culture
authority gradient
The influence of rank differences on communication.reduce the authority gradient
schedule pressure
Pressure to meet a planned operating time.recognize schedule pressure
situational awareness
Understanding the relevant conditions and their implications.maintain situational awareness
unidentified substance
Material whose identity has not been established.report an unidentified substance
direct observation
Something personally seen or otherwise perceived.distinguish direct observation
qualified assessment
Evaluation by personnel with the required competence and authority.request a qualified assessment
hold point
A stage beyond which activity cannot proceed without a required condition.respect the hold point
closed-loop communication
Communication in which receipt and understanding are confirmed.use closed-loop communication
residual risk
Risk remaining after the relevant controls are applied.assess residual risk

The task is already stopped in this fictional case. Do not confuse a later written report with the immediate escalation, or acknowledgement with permission to restart. Only the specified authorized process can change the current task status.

C Notice the language

Directness can be professional care

A clear stop-work statement may sound abrupt to someone accustomed to soft requests. In the supplied case, the action boundary needs to be unmistakable. Explain the observation and use the local escalation route; do not weaken the message to protect a senior colleague from mild discomfort.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the action clearly
The affected task is stopped under our local process.
Locate the observation
I saw a wet patch beside the equipment at Stand 6.
Give the observation time
I observed it at 08:40 local time.
Separate observation and cause
I have not identified the substance or its source.
Request qualified review
The equipment team needs to assess the condition.
Avoid an unsafe assumption
We cannot treat it as harmless because the schedule is tight.
Name the restart boundary
Restart requires the designated supervisor's authorization.
Check understanding
Please confirm that the task remains stopped.
Avoid self-assigned repair
I am not authorized to handle the substance or repair the equipment.
Keep the report factual
No injury or equipment fault has been established.
Respond to pressure
The delay does not remove the assessment requirement.
Acknowledge the report
Thank you for raising the concern promptly.
Name the next responsibility
I will coordinate the required assessment.
Keep acknowledgement separate
I have received the report; that is not restart approval.
Preserve the record
Record the time, location, observation, and action taken.
Close the loop
We will communicate any authorized change in status explicitly.
6 language and precision notes
Stop versus perhaps pause
Do not soften an active safety instruction into an optional suggestion.
Observed
Identifies firsthand information, not a diagnosis.
Unidentified
Keeps a missing fact visible.
Local time
States the clock basis so later records can be compared.
Acknowledged
Means received, not approved for continuation.
Until authorized
Defines the boundary for resuming, not an estimated delay.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which message best preserves the facts?

Answer and explanation

B. Task stopped: unidentified wet patch observed beside equipment at Stand 6, 08:40 local.

The correct message gives the observation, location, time, and actual task status without inventing a cause.

2. Which response to pressure is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

D. The task remains stopped until the required assessment and restart authorization.

The local process requires assessment and authorization regardless of schedule pressure.

3. What does a supervisor's acknowledgement establish?

Answer and explanation

D. The concern has been received.

Receipt of the report does not establish the technical findings or restart decision.

4. Which statement about reporting is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Record the observation and action without assigning an unverified cause.

Factual reporting preserves evidence; the case provides no universal immunity or confirmed fault.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

A concern must remain visible under schedule pressure

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dana: Ramp agent / Malik: Ground-operations supervisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • hazard report
  • direct observation
  • qualified assessment
  • authority gradient
  • Schedule pressure
  • unidentified substance
  • situational awareness
  • hold point
  • closed-loop communication
  • restart authorization
  1. Dana
    The affected task at Stand 6 is stopped under our local process. I saw a wet patch beside the ground equipment at eight forty local time.
  2. Malik
    I have received the 1. Thank you for raising it. Keep the task status clear while I coordinate the required assessment. Have you identified the material or its source?
  3. Dana
    No. I observed the patch, but I cannot identify the substance. I have not handled it or attempted a repair outside my role.
  4. Malik
    That distinction between 2 and interpretation matters. We should record what you saw and the action taken, without filling the missing information with a guessed fault.
  5. Dana
    A colleague says the turnaround is already behind schedule and suggests continuing while the equipment team checks. I need a clear response to that suggestion.
  6. Malik
    3 does not remove the hold. Under this process, the affected task remains stopped until the assessment is complete and the designated supervisor authorizes restart.
  7. Dana
    I will tell the team the task is still stopped; the patch has not been identified.
  8. Malik
    Refer to the 4 requirement. Name the task and current status, then direct questions through the responsible supervisor. You do not need to accuse anyone to keep the instruction firm.
  9. Dana
    Should I describe the patch as hydraulic fluid in the written record? It is beside equipment, but nobody has confirmed what it is.
  10. Malik
    Use 5 or simply describe the wet patch. Location beside equipment does not establish its source. The qualified team can supply verified findings through the proper record.
  11. Dana
    Understood. I will give the stand, observation time, visible condition, and the fact that I invoked the local stop-work process. No injury is established.
  12. Malik
    That supports 6 without inventing a diagnosis. It also helps the incoming staff understand what remains unresolved, especially if the handover occurs before the assessment is complete.
  13. Dana
    The senior agent asked whether I was questioning his experience. I am not; I need him to understand that nobody has authorized a restart.
  14. Malik
    The 7 should not hide the concern. You can be courteous and explicit: the task is stopped under the local process, and restart authorization is still required.
  15. Dana
    I will use that wording. I will also ask the receiving person to repeat the task status so that a brief acknowledgement is not mistaken for agreement to continue.
  16. Malik
    That is 8. Check that they understood the affected task and the restart condition. A quick response such as noted is not always enough to show that shared understanding.
  17. Dana
    Once the equipment team has assessed the patch, I will wait for the designated supervisor's decision rather than infer permission from people returning to the area.
  18. Malik
    Correct. The 9 informs the decision, but it is not an unspoken release. Any authorized change must be communicated explicitly through the local process.
  19. Dana
    For now, the affected task remains stopped. The substance and source are unconfirmed, assessment is pending, and nobody has communicated restart authorization.
  20. Malik
    That is the correct 10. I will coordinate the assessment and keep the relevant teams informed. Do not substitute the turnaround target for the required decision.

Answers and explanations
  1. hazard report Turn 2. The hazard report communicates the observed potentially harmful condition; receiving it does not establish the cause or authorize restart.
  2. direct observation Turn 4. Direct observation is what Dana personally saw, as distinct from an inferred substance, equipment fault, or cause.
  3. Schedule pressure Turn 6. Schedule pressure explains the urgency to finish but does not satisfy the assessment or restart requirements.
  4. restart authorization Turn 8. Restart authorization is the required decision after assessment, not a choice made informally by a colleague under time pressure.
  5. unidentified substance Turn 10. Unidentified substance preserves the missing identification instead of labeling the material as a specific fluid without evidence.
  6. situational awareness Turn 12. Situational awareness depends on accurate relevant conditions and status, not additional details that have not been established.
  7. authority gradient Turn 14. The authority gradient is the effect of rank differences on speaking up; it should not conceal the active safety boundary.
  8. closed-loop communication Turn 16. Closed-loop communication confirms receipt and understanding, reducing the risk that a safety message is heard but interpreted incorrectly.
  9. qualified assessment Turn 18. A qualified assessment supplies the relevant evaluation, but the separate authorized restart decision must still be communicated.
  10. hold point Turn 20. The hold point is the boundary that remains in place until the specified assessment and authorization requirements are satisfied.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dana: The affected task at Stand 6 is stopped under our local process. I saw a wet patch beside the ground equipment at eight forty local time.
  2. Malik: I have received the hazard report. Thank you for raising it. Keep the task status clear while I coordinate the required assessment. Have you identified the material or its source?
  3. Dana: No. I observed the patch, but I cannot identify the substance. I have not handled it or attempted a repair outside my role.
  4. Malik: That distinction between direct observation and interpretation matters. We should record what you saw and the action taken, without filling the missing information with a guessed fault.
  5. Dana: A colleague says the turnaround is already behind schedule and suggests continuing while the equipment team checks. I need a clear response to that suggestion.
  6. Malik: Schedule pressure does not remove the hold. Under this process, the affected task remains stopped until the assessment is complete and the designated supervisor authorizes restart.
  7. Dana: I will tell the team the task is still stopped; the patch has not been identified.
  8. Malik: Refer to the restart authorization requirement. Name the task and current status, then direct questions through the responsible supervisor. You do not need to accuse anyone to keep the instruction firm.
  9. Dana: Should I describe the patch as hydraulic fluid in the written record? It is beside equipment, but nobody has confirmed what it is.
  10. Malik: Use unidentified substance or simply describe the wet patch. Location beside equipment does not establish its source. The qualified team can supply verified findings through the proper record.
  11. Dana: Understood. I will give the stand, observation time, visible condition, and the fact that I invoked the local stop-work process. No injury is established.
  12. Malik: That supports situational awareness without inventing a diagnosis. It also helps the incoming staff understand what remains unresolved, especially if the handover occurs before the assessment is complete.
  13. Dana: The senior agent asked whether I was questioning his experience. I am not; I need him to understand that nobody has authorized a restart.
  14. Malik: The authority gradient should not hide the concern. You can be courteous and explicit: the task is stopped under the local process, and restart authorization is still required.
  15. Dana: I will use that wording. I will also ask the receiving person to repeat the task status so that a brief acknowledgement is not mistaken for agreement to continue.
  16. Malik: That is closed-loop communication. Check that they understood the affected task and the restart condition. A quick response such as noted is not always enough to show that shared understanding.
  17. Dana: Once the equipment team has assessed the patch, I will wait for the designated supervisor's decision rather than infer permission from people returning to the area.
  18. Malik: Correct. The qualified assessment informs the decision, but it is not an unspoken release. Any authorized change must be communicated explicitly through the local process.
  19. Dana: For now, the affected task remains stopped. The substance and source are unconfirmed, assessment is pending, and nobody has communicated restart authorization.
  20. Malik: That is the correct hold point. I will coordinate the assessment and keep the relevant teams informed. Do not substitute the turnaround target for the required decision.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Request a hazard review near the cart

Fictional ground-operations language simulation, not operational phraseology: a worker observes loose material near a service cart; local safety procedures govern all actions.

  1. Ground services agent: I can see loose material beside the service cart. Please pause this task for a hazard review.
  2. Ground services supervisor: I hear your concern. Can you identify the location without approaching the equipment?
  3. Ground services agent: It's beside the cart marked C7. I haven't inspected it or established what it is.
  4. Ground services supervisor: Thank you. We'll use the local stop-work and reporting process, not make assumptions.
  5. Ground services agent: Could you confirm who will receive the report and coordinate the authorized assessment?
  6. Ground services supervisor: I'll receive it and contact the designated safety lead through our established process.
  7. Ground services agent: Should I describe the material as a confirmed equipment leak in the report?
  8. Ground services supervisor: No. Describe what you observed and keep the cause unconfirmed until assessed.
  9. Ground services agent: Understood. I'll report the location and observation, without claiming a technical diagnosis.
  10. Ground services supervisor: I'll confirm receipt. Speaking up with a specific concern supports our reporting culture.
A report needs an owner

Separate fictional safety-management exercise: a training report about poor lighting was submitted yesterday but has no assigned reviewer; no operational clearance is being discussed.

  1. Safety coordinator: Yesterday's lighting hazard report has a reference number, but no reviewer is assigned.
  2. Department manager: I saw it in the safety management system. Isn't submission enough to start follow-up?
  3. Safety coordinator: Submission records the concern. We still need an identified person to assess and respond.
  4. Department manager: What exactly are you asking me to arrange before the afternoon coordination meeting?
  5. Safety coordinator: Please identify the authorized reviewer and confirm who will update the person who reported it.
  6. Department manager: I can request that assignment now, but I can't promise the assessment will finish today.
  7. Safety coordinator: That's fine. The immediate request is ownership confirmation, not an unsupported completion deadline.
  8. Department manager: Should we close the report once a reviewer accepts it in the system?
  9. Safety coordinator: No. Acceptance and resolution are different; closure follows the applicable process and evidence.
  10. Department manager: I'll seek the assignment and report its status at the meeting, including anything still pending.
Make space for a contractor's concern

Separate fictional induction exercise: a contractor reports that a briefing diagram is unreadable; the team has not started the represented task.

  1. Contractor liaison: Our contractor cannot read the diagram in the task briefing. Please provide a legible approved copy.
  2. Training supervisor: Which part is unclear? I want the request to reach the correct document owner.
  3. Contractor liaison: The equipment labels blur when enlarged. We shouldn't ask the contractor to guess their meaning.
  4. Training supervisor: Agreed. I'll request the current readable version from document control before this exercise continues.
  5. Contractor liaison: Could you also confirm that asking for clarification won't be treated as poor preparation?
  6. Training supervisor: Yes. Our training discussion should support reporting concerns, not discourage questions about unclear information.
  7. Contractor liaison: Someone suggested using an old printed diagram because its labels are easier to read.
  8. Training supervisor: Readability alone doesn't establish that it's current. We need a verified version as well.
  9. Contractor liaison: I'll explain that the request concerns both legibility and version status, not just printing.
  10. Training supervisor: Good. We'll resume the language exercise with verified materials, without implying authorization for actual work.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A concern must remain visible under schedule pressure
  1. Read turns 1-10 with the corrected words; emphasize the task, stand, time, and unidentified condition.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20; keep the stop statement direct and the assessment separate from restart authorization.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Report the condition, not a guessed fault

At Stand 9, a worker observes an unidentified patch at 07:20 local. The affected task is stopped under the local process. Assessment and restart authorization are pending.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Worker: "The observation was at Stand ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 9

Stand nine is the supplied location of the observed condition.

2. Supervisor: "The time was ___ local."

Answer and explanation

B. 07:20

The brief gives seven twenty on the local clock.

3. Worker: "The substance remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unidentified

No assessment has established the material's identity or source.

4. Supervisor: "The task remains stopped pending the required restart ___."

Answer and explanation

B. authorization

The local process requires an authorized decision before resuming.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Safety Culture and Stop-Work Authority
Original fictional case: At 08:40 local time, ramp agent Dana observes an unidentified wet patch beside ground equipment at Stand 6. Dana invokes the fictional site's local stop-work process for the affected task and informs supervisor Malik. The equipment team has not assessed the patch or its source. Under this local process, the affected task stays stopped until the designated supervisor completes the required assessment and authorizes restart. Dana is not authorized to handle the substance or repair equipment. A colleague suggests continuing because the turnaround is behind schedule. No injury or equipment fault has been established.
Language workshop: A concern must remain visible under schedule pressure
Communication goal: Use a direct stop-work message, describe an observation, and preserve the authorized restart boundary.
Language guidance: Dana can report a wet patch without naming the substance or declaring an equipment failure. The observation warrants the local process, but it does not establish cause, injury, or the correct technical remedy.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The affected task is stopped under our local process. / I saw a wet patch beside the equipment at Stand 6. / I observed it at 08:40 local time. / I have not identified the substance or its source. / The equipment team needs to assess the condition. / We cannot treat it as harmless because the schedule is tight. / Restart requires the designated supervisor's authorization. / Please confirm that the task remains stopped. / I am not authorized to handle the substance or repair the equipment. / No injury or equipment fault has been established. / The delay does not remove the assessment requirement. / Thank you for raising the concern promptly. / I will coordinate the required assessment. / I have received the report; that is not restart approval. / Record the time, location, observation, and action taken. / We will communicate any authorized change in status explicitly.
Vocabulary: ramp: The airport area used for aircraft servicing and related ground activity. / stand: A designated aircraft parking position. / ground support equipment (GSE): Equipment used to support aircraft ground operations. / hazard: A condition or object with potential to cause harm. / safety risk: The assessed likelihood and severity of harm from a hazard. / stop-work authority: Authority under the relevant process to halt an unsafe or uncertain task. / affected task: The specific activity subject to the concern or restriction. / escalation route: The defined path for raising a concern to the responsible people. / restart authorization: Permission to resume after the required conditions are satisfied. / safety management system (SMS): An organized approach to managing safety risks and controls. / hazard report: A record communicating a potentially harmful condition. / risk control: A measure intended to reduce or manage risk. / safety assurance: Checking whether safety arrangements remain effective. / safety promotion: Training and communication that support safe working practices. / just culture: An approach balancing fair reporting, learning, and accountability. / authority gradient: The influence of rank differences on communication. / schedule pressure: Pressure to meet a planned operating time. / situational awareness: Understanding the relevant conditions and their implications. / unidentified substance: Material whose identity has not been established. / direct observation: Something personally seen or otherwise perceived. / qualified assessment: Evaluation by personnel with the required competence and authority. / hold point: A stage beyond which activity cannot proceed without a required condition. / closed-loop communication: Communication in which receipt and understanding are confirmed. / residual risk: Risk remaining after the relevant controls are applied.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 with the corrected words; emphasize the task, stand, time, and unidentified condition. Switch roles for turns 11-20; keep the stop statement direct and the assessment separate from restart authorization. Read the checked Stand 9 exchange twice, switching roles. Retain 07:20 local and the pending authorization.
Partner: Dana: Ramp agent / Malik: Ground-operations supervisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At Stand 9, a worker observes an unidentified patch at 07:20 local. The affected task is stopped under the local process. Assessment and restart authorization are pending.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dana: Once the equipment team has assessed the patch, I will wait for the designated supervisor's decision rather than infer permission from people returning to the area.
Malik: Correct. The qualified assessment informs the decision, but it is not an unspoken release. Any authorized change must be communicated explicitly through the local process.
Dana: For now, the affected task remains stopped. The substance and source are unconfirmed, assessment is pending, and nobody has communicated restart authorization.
Malik: That is the correct hold point. I will coordinate the assessment and keep the relevant teams informed. Do not substitute the turnaround target for the required decision.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Safety Culture and Stop-Work Authority
Original fictional case: At 08:40 local time, ramp agent Dana observes an unidentified wet patch beside ground equipment at Stand 6. Dana invokes the fictional site's local stop-work process for the affected task and informs supervisor Malik. The equipment team has not assessed the patch or its source. Under this local process, the affected task stays stopped until the designated supervisor completes the required assessment and authorizes restart. Dana is not authorized to handle the substance or repair equipment. A colleague suggests continuing because the turnaround is behind schedule. No injury or equipment fault has been established.
Language workshop: A concern must remain visible under schedule pressure
Communication goal: Use a direct stop-work message, describe an observation, and preserve the authorized restart boundary.
Language guidance: Dana can report a wet patch without naming the substance or declaring an equipment failure. The observation warrants the local process, but it does not establish cause, injury, or the correct technical remedy.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The affected task is stopped under our local process. / I saw a wet patch beside the equipment at Stand 6. / I observed it at 08:40 local time. / I have not identified the substance or its source. / The equipment team needs to assess the condition. / We cannot treat it as harmless because the schedule is tight. / Restart requires the designated supervisor's authorization. / Please confirm that the task remains stopped. / I am not authorized to handle the substance or repair the equipment. / No injury or equipment fault has been established. / The delay does not remove the assessment requirement. / Thank you for raising the concern promptly. / I will coordinate the required assessment. / I have received the report; that is not restart approval. / Record the time, location, observation, and action taken. / We will communicate any authorized change in status explicitly.
Vocabulary: ramp: The airport area used for aircraft servicing and related ground activity. / stand: A designated aircraft parking position. / ground support equipment (GSE): Equipment used to support aircraft ground operations. / hazard: A condition or object with potential to cause harm. / safety risk: The assessed likelihood and severity of harm from a hazard. / stop-work authority: Authority under the relevant process to halt an unsafe or uncertain task. / affected task: The specific activity subject to the concern or restriction. / escalation route: The defined path for raising a concern to the responsible people. / restart authorization: Permission to resume after the required conditions are satisfied. / safety management system (SMS): An organized approach to managing safety risks and controls. / hazard report: A record communicating a potentially harmful condition. / risk control: A measure intended to reduce or manage risk. / safety assurance: Checking whether safety arrangements remain effective. / safety promotion: Training and communication that support safe working practices. / just culture: An approach balancing fair reporting, learning, and accountability. / authority gradient: The influence of rank differences on communication. / schedule pressure: Pressure to meet a planned operating time. / situational awareness: Understanding the relevant conditions and their implications. / unidentified substance: Material whose identity has not been established. / direct observation: Something personally seen or otherwise perceived. / qualified assessment: Evaluation by personnel with the required competence and authority. / hold point: A stage beyond which activity cannot proceed without a required condition. / closed-loop communication: Communication in which receipt and understanding are confirmed. / residual risk: Risk remaining after the relevant controls are applied.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 with the corrected words; emphasize the task, stand, time, and unidentified condition. Switch roles for turns 11-20; keep the stop statement direct and the assessment separate from restart authorization. Read the checked Stand 9 exchange twice, switching roles. Retain 07:20 local and the pending authorization.
Partner: Dana: Ramp agent / Malik: Ground-operations supervisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At Stand 9, a worker observes an unidentified patch at 07:20 local. The affected task is stopped under the local process. Assessment and restart authorization are pending.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dana: Once the equipment team has assessed the patch, I will wait for the designated supervisor's decision rather than infer permission from people returning to the area.
Malik: Correct. The qualified assessment informs the decision, but it is not an unspoken release. Any authorized change must be communicated explicitly through the local process.
Dana: For now, the affected task remains stopped. The substance and source are unconfirmed, assessment is pending, and nobody has communicated restart authorization.
Malik: That is the correct hold point. I will coordinate the assessment and keep the relevant teams informed. Do not substitute the turnaround target for the required decision.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Operations Control and Dispatch CoordinationA provisional plan was labeled confirmed+

Your goal

Give a timestamped operations summary that preserves separate aircraft and crew statuses.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

A provisional plan was labeled confirmed

At 10:10 UTC, a coordination snapshot for fictional Flight EL204 lists fleet aircraft A12 as provisional. Crew control has not confirmed the assigned crew's status. The planning target is an 11:00 UTC off-block time, not a cleared departure. Planner Nina finds a draft message calling the aircraft, crew, and departure confirmed. Operations coordinator Joel will seek aircraft confirmation, while crew controller Sara owns the crew-status check. Both will provide their next status at 10:25 UTC. These are two tracked planning items, not an exhaustive release checklist; all other approved operating processes still apply.

Nina: Operations planner / Joel: Operations coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the aircraft assignment's status at 10:10 UTC?

Answer and explanation

A. Provisional

The snapshot explicitly labels fleet aircraft A12 as a provisional assignment.

2. What does 11:00 UTC represent?

Answer and explanation

B. A planning target for off-block time

The brief labels eleven hundred as a planning target, not an operational clearance.

3. Who owns the crew-status check?

Answer and explanation

B. Sara in crew control

Sara is the named crew controller responsible for that unresolved planning item.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Aircraft A12 is provisional, crew status is unconfirmed, and 11:00 UTC is a planning target. Keep all three qualifiers in a short update. Replacing them with confirmed changes the operational meaning of the message.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
operations control center (OCC)
The unit coordinating an airline's operational activity.contact the operations control center
operational control
Responsibility for decisions about initiating, conducting, or ending a flight under applicable rules.distinguish operational control
dispatch coordination
Communication among the relevant flight-planning and operating functions.coordinate with dispatch
aircraft assignment
Allocation of a particular aircraft to an operation.verify the aircraft assignment
fleet identifier
An internal label identifying an aircraft in the fleet.confirm the fleet identifier
provisional plan
A proposed arrangement that remains subject to confirmation.label a provisional plan
crew control
The function coordinating crew assignments and related status.contact crew control
crew status
The verified position of the relevant crew requirements or assignment.confirm crew status
crew roster
The schedule of assigned crew duties.check the crew roster
duty period
A defined period of crew duty under applicable rules.verify the duty period
qualification
A required credential or demonstrated competence for a role.verify crew qualifications
positioning
Moving crew or aircraft to a required location.coordinate crew positioning
off-block time
The time an aircraft leaves its parking position.distinguish off-block time
estimated off-block time (EOBT)
The estimated time the aircraft will leave its parking position.update the estimated off-block time
target time
A planning objective rather than proof of completion or clearance.label the target time
UTC
Coordinated Universal Time, a common aviation time reference.state times in UTC
timestamp
A recorded time attached to information or an event.preserve the timestamp
status snapshot
A report of known conditions at a particular time.issue a status snapshot
dependency
A condition or input on which a later action relies.track an unresolved dependency
operating restriction
A limitation applying to an operation.communicate operating restrictions
flight release
The applicable authorized release documentation or decision for an operation.verify flight release status
contingency aircraft
An alternative aircraft considered if the original plan cannot proceed.assess a contingency aircraft
confirmation source
The responsible origin of a verified status.identify the confirmation source
planning assumption
A condition treated as a basis for a plan but not yet established.expose a planning assumption

The 10:25 UTC check-in is a time for new status information, not a guarantee that both items will be resolved. Aircraft and crew confirmation are not presented as the complete set of conditions for an actual flight release.

C Notice the language

One label cannot describe several dependencies

A plan can look complete because every field contains text. Separate the status of each dependency before using an overall label. A colleague who asks for the source and timestamp is checking the meaning of confirmed, not refusing to cooperate with the plan.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Give the time basis
This is the status as of 10:10 UTC.
Label the aircraft
A12 remains provisionally assigned.
Label the crew item
Crew confirmation is still pending.
Correct the draft
Confirmed is not supported by this snapshot.
Describe the target
The planning target is 11:00 UTC off-block.
Avoid release language
That target is not a departure clearance.
Name the aircraft owner
Joel is checking the aircraft assignment.
Name the crew owner
Sara in crew control owns the crew-status check.
Give the next check-in
Both owners will update at 10:25 UTC.
Keep resolution uncertain
The next update may still show an unresolved item.
Preserve the source
Please include who confirmed each status.
Avoid a stale message
Replace the earlier draft with the timestamped correction.
Separate dependencies
Aircraft confirmation does not confirm the crew.
Keep other requirements visible
All other approved operating processes still apply.
Ask for a readback
Please repeat the target and the two current statuses.
Close the coordination
We will revise the plan when verified information changes.
6 language and precision notes
Provisional
A proposed assignment, not a completed commitment.
As of
Limits the statement to the recorded information time.
Target
Names an objective, not clearance or actual movement.
UTC
Keep the time basis explicit; do not silently mix local times.
Pending
Makes an unresolved item visible.
Confirmed by
Identifies the responsible source behind a status claim.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which short update is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. At 10:10 UTC, A12 is provisional, crew is pending, and 11:00 is the off-block target.

The correct update preserves the separate statuses and the planning nature of the time.

2. What does aircraft confirmation alone establish?

Answer and explanation

A. The aircraft assignment's status, not crew confirmation

One dependency's confirmation does not automatically resolve the others.

3. Which phrase should not be used for the 11:00 target?

Answer and explanation

A. Cleared departure

The case provides a target, not an operational authorization to depart.

4. What should happen at 10:25 if crew status remains unresolved?

Answer and explanation

D. Report it as pending with the current timestamp and owner.

An update should accurately preserve the remaining uncertainty rather than force closure.

E Conversations

A provisional plan was labeled confirmed

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nina: Operations planner / Joel: Operations coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • UTC
  • confirmation source
  • Crew control
  • status snapshot
  • aircraft assignment
  • target time
  • timestamp
  • crew status
  • flight release
  • dependency
  1. Nina
    The draft says aircraft, crew, and departure confirmed. That does not match the ten-ten UTC snapshot. Can we check each item before the message goes out?
  2. Joel
    Yes. The 1 for A12 is still provisional. I am checking it, but I have not received the confirmation needed to change that status.
  3. Nina
    The crew field names a team, which may be why the draft looked complete. Does a name in that field mean crew control has confirmed the status?
  4. Joel
    No. 2 has not confirmed it. Sara owns that check. We should show the unresolved status rather than infer readiness from an assigned name.
  5. Nina
    Then the eleven-hundred time also needs a qualifier. Is it our planned off-block time, or has someone authorized a departure at that time?
  6. Joel
    It is a 3 for off-block at eleven hundred UTC. It is not a departure clearance. The draft has combined a planning objective with two unfinished confirmations.
  7. Nina
    Then I will replace confirmed with the separate aircraft, crew, and target labels. Which snapshot time should go at the top?
  8. Joel
    Include the 4 of ten-ten UTC. These statuses may change, and recipients need to know which snapshot they are reading rather than treating every copied message as current.
  9. Nina
    Can I put your name against both checks, or does the crew item need a different contact?
  10. Joel
    I own the aircraft check; Sara owns 5. Both of us will provide a status at ten twenty-five UTC. That is the next check-in, not a promise that both items will be resolved.
  11. Nina
    Suppose you confirm A12 at ten twenty, but Sara is still waiting at ten twenty-five. We should report one confirmed item and one pending item, correct?
  12. Joel
    Correct. Each 6 retains its own status. Do not upgrade the whole plan to confirmed because one favorable answer arrives, or leave the earlier provisional aircraft label unchanged after verification.
  13. Nina
    What should we say about release requirements beyond these two items? I do not want readers to treat our brief planning update as the complete operating checklist.
  14. Joel
    State that the applicable 7 process and all other required checks still apply. This message tracks aircraft and crew coordination; it does not replace authorized operational decisions or approved manuals.
  15. Nina
    I will withdraw the old draft so colleagues do not continue forwarding the unsupported confirmation.
  16. Joel
    Identify the 8 for each later update. If someone asks why a status changed, we should be able to point to verified information rather than a wording preference.
  17. Nina
    There is one more risk: a local station might read eleven hundred on its own clock. Every time in this exchange should retain the same reference.
  18. Joel
    Use 9 throughout. Do not silently translate a number while keeping its original label. The recipient should see the same time basis for the snapshot, target, and next check-in.
  19. Nina
    The revised message reads: as of ten-ten UTC, A12 provisional, crew confirmation pending, target off-block eleven hundred UTC, next owner updates at ten twenty-five UTC.
  20. Joel
    That is a clear 10. Send it as the correction, keep the separate owners visible, and revise it when verified information changes. The overall release boundary remains unchanged.

Answers and explanations
  1. aircraft assignment Turn 2. The aircraft assignment is provisional in the supplied snapshot and must not be described as confirmed.
  2. Crew control Turn 4. Crew control is the responsible function for the crew-status check; a populated planning field does not replace its confirmation.
  3. target time Turn 6. A target time is the planning objective, not an authorized departure or an actual off-block event.
  4. timestamp Turn 8. The timestamp identifies when the reported statuses were known and helps distinguish the correction from an older draft.
  5. crew status Turn 10. Crew status is the separate unresolved item assigned to Sara, not something Joel's aircraft confirmation can settle.
  6. dependency Turn 12. A dependency has its own status; resolving one does not automatically resolve another required planning input.
  7. flight release Turn 14. Flight release involves the relevant authorized operating process and is not established merely by the two planning items discussed here.
  8. confirmation source Turn 16. The confirmation source is the responsible person or record supporting a status change, not the existence of a polished draft.
  9. UTC Turn 18. UTC supplies the common time basis explicitly required in this snapshot, preventing silent substitution of local clock times.
  10. status snapshot Turn 20. The status snapshot summarizes the known position at a stated time without turning assumptions into operational confirmation.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Aircraft assigned provisionally

Fictional operations-control briefing: aircraft routing is provisional and crew status remains unresolved; this is workplace English, not dispatch authorization.

  1. Operations coordinator: The draft says the plan is confirmed, but the aircraft assignment is still provisional.
  2. Dispatch liaison: You're right. We have completed the routing proposal, not the final assignment checks.
  3. Operations coordinator: What about crew status? The gate team is asking whether it can announce a settled plan.
  4. Dispatch liaison: That question remains with the authorized crew team. We cannot determine crew legality here.
  5. Operations coordinator: Then which completed action can I safely include in the coordination update this morning?
  6. Dispatch liaison: Say the routing proposal is prepared, while aircraft confirmation and crew-status verification remain pending.
  7. Operations coordinator: Does the proposed delay code establish why the flight will operate late today?
  8. Dispatch liaison: Not by itself. The draft code also needs verification before it's presented as final.
  9. Operations coordinator: I'll remove confirmed and list the unresolved checks with their responsible teams.
  10. Dispatch liaison: Good. Our next update should report verified changes, without converting planning work into authorization.
A revised route has not reached every team

Separate fictional coordination case: a revised aircraft-routing document is approved for internal planning, but station receipt is unconfirmed; no flight-release decision is involved.

  1. Network planner: The revised routing document is ready. Can I mark the station coordination task complete?
  2. Station coordinator: Not yet. You have approval for the planning document, but our receipt check remains open.
  3. Network planner: I sent it to the distribution list ten minutes ago. Doesn't that establish receipt?
  4. Station coordinator: It establishes sending, not that each relevant station has received and understood the revision.
  5. Network planner: What should I report to operations control while you check the station responses?
  6. Station coordinator: The document is issued for planning; acknowledgment from the affected stations is still pending.
  7. Network planner: Could a missing acknowledgment affect how the previous aircraft assignment is being discussed?
  8. Station coordinator: Yes, teams may still be working from the earlier proposal. We need confirmation, not speculation.
  9. Network planner: I'll keep distribution and acknowledgment as separate items in the dispatch coordination summary.
  10. Station coordinator: I'll gather the responses and flag any team that still needs the current planning version.
Separate the completed review from the open question

Separate fictional desk exercise: a delay summary's timestamps have been checked, but the reason classification remains under review.

  1. Operations analyst: I've checked all timestamps in the delay summary, so the report is ready for release.
  2. Duty coordinator: The timestamps are verified, but the delay code is still marked under review.
  3. Operations analyst: I overlooked that distinction. The timeline is complete; the reason classification remains unresolved.
  4. Duty coordinator: Exactly. What effect does that have on the summary we give the station manager?
  5. Operations analyst: We can explain the recorded sequence, but shouldn't present the proposed cause as confirmed.
  6. Duty coordinator: Does the dispatch liaison have responsibility for obtaining the classification decision in this exercise?
  7. Operations analyst: Yes, that assignment is listed. Their response hasn't arrived, so I'll keep it pending.
  8. Duty coordinator: Please make the next update specific instead of saying everything is almost finished.
  9. Operations analyst: I'll report verified timestamps, the open delay code, and the liaison responsible for its review.
  10. Duty coordinator: That gives the manager a usable status without implying that the entire report has approval.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Operations Control and Dispatch Coordination
Original fictional case: At 10:10 UTC, a coordination snapshot for fictional Flight EL204 lists fleet aircraft A12 as provisional. Crew control has not confirmed the assigned crew's status. The planning target is an 11:00 UTC off-block time, not a cleared departure. Planner Nina finds a draft message calling the aircraft, crew, and departure confirmed. Operations coordinator Joel will seek aircraft confirmation, while crew controller Sara owns the crew-status check. Both will provide their next status at 10:25 UTC. These are two tracked planning items, not an exhaustive release checklist; all other approved operating processes still apply.
Language workshop: A provisional plan was labeled confirmed
Communication goal: Give a timestamped operations summary that preserves separate aircraft and crew statuses.
Language guidance: Aircraft A12 is provisional, crew status is unconfirmed, and 11:00 UTC is a planning target. Keep all three qualifiers in a short update. Replacing them with confirmed changes the operational meaning of the message.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This is the status as of 10:10 UTC. / A12 remains provisionally assigned. / Crew confirmation is still pending. / Confirmed is not supported by this snapshot. / The planning target is 11:00 UTC off-block. / That target is not a departure clearance. / Joel is checking the aircraft assignment. / Sara in crew control owns the crew-status check. / Both owners will update at 10:25 UTC. / The next update may still show an unresolved item. / Please include who confirmed each status. / Replace the earlier draft with the timestamped correction. / Aircraft confirmation does not confirm the crew. / All other approved operating processes still apply. / Please repeat the target and the two current statuses. / We will revise the plan when verified information changes.
Vocabulary: operations control center (OCC): The unit coordinating an airline's operational activity. / operational control: Responsibility for decisions about initiating, conducting, or ending a flight under applicable rules. / dispatch coordination: Communication among the relevant flight-planning and operating functions. / aircraft assignment: Allocation of a particular aircraft to an operation. / fleet identifier: An internal label identifying an aircraft in the fleet. / provisional plan: A proposed arrangement that remains subject to confirmation. / crew control: The function coordinating crew assignments and related status. / crew status: The verified position of the relevant crew requirements or assignment. / crew roster: The schedule of assigned crew duties. / duty period: A defined period of crew duty under applicable rules. / qualification: A required credential or demonstrated competence for a role. / positioning: Moving crew or aircraft to a required location. / off-block time: The time an aircraft leaves its parking position. / estimated off-block time (EOBT): The estimated time the aircraft will leave its parking position. / target time: A planning objective rather than proof of completion or clearance. / UTC: Coordinated Universal Time, a common aviation time reference. / timestamp: A recorded time attached to information or an event. / status snapshot: A report of known conditions at a particular time. / dependency: A condition or input on which a later action relies. / operating restriction: A limitation applying to an operation. / flight release: The applicable authorized release documentation or decision for an operation. / contingency aircraft: An alternative aircraft considered if the original plan cannot proceed. / confirmation source: The responsible origin of a verified status. / planning assumption: A condition treated as a basis for a plan but not yet established.
Speaking task: Read the corrected dialogue, saying the snapshot, target, and check-in times with UTC each time. Switch roles and repeat turns 9-14. Stress Joel versus Sara and one confirmed item versus the whole plan. Read the checked transfer twice. Keep B7 provisional, crew pending, and 13:00 a target rather than clearance.
Partner: Nina: Operations planner / Joel: Operations coordinator

Optional second-round challenge: At 12:05 UTC, aircraft B7 is provisional and crew confirmation is pending. Target off-block is 13:00 UTC. The next coordination update is 12:20 UTC; no release is established.

Joel: Use UTC throughout. Do not silently translate a number while keeping its original label. The recipient should see the same time basis for the snapshot, target, and next check-in.
Nina: The revised message reads: as of ten-ten UTC, A12 provisional, crew confirmation pending, target off-block eleven hundred UTC, next owner updates at ten twenty-five UTC.
Joel: That is a clear status snapshot. Send it as the correction, keep the separate owners visible, and revise it when verified information changes. The overall release boundary remains unchanged.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
