E Conversations

A handover was sent, but nobody accepted it

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Hassan: Turnaround coordinator / Elin: Incoming ground-services lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Task completion
  • outstanding balance
  • service handover
  • truck arrival
  • status report
  • handover gap
  • required quantity
  • confirmed receipt
  • task acceptance
  • delivery note
  1. Hassan
    The outgoing team sent the catering handover, but I cannot find a receiving person's acceptance. Before they leave, can we confirm the task and current balance?
  2. Elin
    Yes. Give me the 1 and what has actually been received. I do not want to accept a vague instruction that assumes the delivery is already complete.
  3. Hassan
    We require ninety-six packs. The receiving record confirms eighty. A truck is here with a note listing the other sixteen, but that receipt has not been verified.
  4. Elin
    Then the 2 is sixteen. The truck's presence does not change the confirmed count. I will keep that distinction when I speak to the receiving team.
  5. Hassan
    Someone marked the task nearly done after seeing the vehicle. We need a status that different teams will interpret consistently.
  6. Elin
    We can say 3 is confirmed while receipt verification remains open. Those are separate milestones. A supplier's arrival is useful information, but it is not the completed receiving step.
  7. Hassan
    The driver has a delivery note for sixteen. Can you check it against the receipt before we change our total?
  8. Elin
    Correct. I will compare the 4 with the receiving information through the approved process. Until that verification is recorded, the current confirmed total remains eighty, not ninety-six.
  9. Hassan
    Will you own that verification and the next report? The earlier handover went to a group address without explicit acceptance.
  10. Elin
    I accept the 5 for checking the remaining sixteen and reporting the result. Please record me as the receiving lead so the next shift knows who has the task.
  11. Hassan
    I will record that acceptance now. Our next coordination check is thirteen thirty-five UTC, with thirteen forty-five still the planning target for off-block.
  12. Elin
    I will give a 6 at thirteen thirty-five UTC. That is a commitment to report the verified position, not a promise that the remaining delivery will be complete by that time.
  13. Hassan
    Thank you. If only part of the sixteen is verified, report that number and the balance. Do not mark all ninety-six received just to close the task.
  14. Elin
    I will update 7 from evidence. If all sixteen are verified, the balance becomes zero. If fewer are verified, I will state the remaining amount rather than mark the task complete.
  15. Hassan
    And we should not use catering complete as shorthand for the entire aircraft being ready. Other teams still have their own required checks and responsibilities.
  16. Elin
    Agreed. 8 here is limited to the catering activity. I will not use it as a flight-release statement or as proof that unrelated ground-service work has finished.
  17. Hassan
    We should review why the earlier handover had no acceptance, using the messages instead of immediately blaming a department.
  18. Elin
    Yes. The 9 is established, but its cause is not. Preserve the messages and review the process. Solving the current ownership problem does not settle the later causal question.
  19. Hassan
    Let me read back the current arrangement: ninety-six required, eighty confirmed received, sixteen pending verification, Elin responsible, and a status report at thirteen thirty-five UTC.
  20. Elin
    That captures my 10. Keep thirteen forty-five labeled as the off-block target. I will report the verified catering balance and any remaining exception at the agreed check-in.

Answers and explanations
  1. required quantity Turn 2. The required quantity is ninety-six packs, the reference against which confirmed receipt and the outstanding balance are calculated.
  2. outstanding balance Turn 4. The outstanding balance remains sixteen because only eighty of the required ninety-six have confirmed receipt.
  3. truck arrival Turn 6. Truck arrival is a verified milestone of vehicle presence, not proof of the remaining goods' receipt or task completion.
  4. delivery note Turn 8. The delivery note states the supplier's claimed quantity, which the receiving process still needs to verify.
  5. service handover Turn 10. The service handover transfers the specified verification and reporting responsibility once Elin explicitly accepts it.
  6. status report Turn 12. The status report is the commitment due at thirteen thirty-five; it does not guarantee that delivery will be complete by then.
  7. confirmed receipt Turn 14. Confirmed receipt changes only when the receiving process verifies additional packs, not when schedule pressure increases.
  8. Task completion Turn 16. Task completion concerns this specified catering activity only and does not establish completion of all other turnaround or release requirements.
  9. handover gap Turn 18. The handover gap is the missing named acceptance; its existence does not by itself prove who caused it or why.
  10. task acceptance Turn 20. Task acceptance means Elin has taken responsibility for verification and reporting, not that the goods are already accepted or the task finished.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Hassan: The outgoing team sent the catering handover, but I cannot find a receiving person's acceptance. Before they leave, can we confirm the task and current balance?
  2. Elin: Yes. Give me the required quantity and what has actually been received. I do not want to accept a vague instruction that assumes the delivery is already complete.
  3. Hassan: We require ninety-six packs. The receiving record confirms eighty. A truck is here with a note listing the other sixteen, but that receipt has not been verified.
  4. Elin: Then the outstanding balance is sixteen. The truck's presence does not change the confirmed count. I will keep that distinction when I speak to the receiving team.
  5. Hassan: Someone marked the task nearly done after seeing the vehicle. We need a status that different teams will interpret consistently.
  6. Elin: We can say truck arrival is confirmed while receipt verification remains open. Those are separate milestones. A supplier's arrival is useful information, but it is not the completed receiving step.
  7. Hassan: The driver has a delivery note for sixteen. Can you check it against the receipt before we change our total?
  8. Elin: Correct. I will compare the delivery note with the receiving information through the approved process. Until that verification is recorded, the current confirmed total remains eighty, not ninety-six.
  9. Hassan: Will you own that verification and the next report? The earlier handover went to a group address without explicit acceptance.
  10. Elin: I accept the service handover for checking the remaining sixteen and reporting the result. Please record me as the receiving lead so the next shift knows who has the task.
  11. Hassan: I will record that acceptance now. Our next coordination check is thirteen thirty-five UTC, with thirteen forty-five still the planning target for off-block.
  12. Elin: I will give a status report at thirteen thirty-five UTC. That is a commitment to report the verified position, not a promise that the remaining delivery will be complete by that time.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Who owns the open service task?

Fictional turnaround handoff: a cabin-service task is open with no receiving owner; the dialogue transfers coordination only, not aircraft-release authority.

  1. Outgoing turnaround coordinator: The cabin-service task remains open, and the handoff note doesn't name anyone who accepted it.
  2. Incoming turnaround coordinator: I'll take coordination follow-up. Has the service provider reported completion anywhere in the record?
  3. Outgoing turnaround coordinator: No completion report is attached. The last entry says the request was sent to ground handling.
  4. Incoming turnaround coordinator: Then I won't describe sending as acceptance. I'll contact the provider for a verified status.
  5. Outgoing turnaround coordinator: Please also confirm the person who accepts the task, so the next handoff isn't ambiguous.
  6. Incoming turnaround coordinator: Understood. Does this open item tell us anything about the load sheet's current approval status?
  7. Outgoing turnaround coordinator: No. That's a separate process and isn't established by the cabin-service record we're discussing.
  8. Incoming turnaround coordinator: I'll keep those items separate and avoid making any operational release statement during the update.
  9. Outgoing turnaround coordinator: Can you repeat what you're taking over before I finish my coordination shift?
  10. Incoming turnaround coordinator: Provider contact, task acceptance, and status follow-up; the service remains open until verified through the process.
A provider acknowledgment without completion

Separate fictional desk case: a ground-handling provider acknowledged a service request at 14:10; completion has not been reported.

  1. Ground handling liaison: The provider acknowledged our request at 14:10. I'm handing the follow-up to your shift.
  2. Turnaround supervisor: I accept the follow-up. To be clear, acknowledgment doesn't mean the requested service is complete.
  3. Ground handling liaison: Correct. There is no completion message in the record available to us at this handoff.
  4. Turnaround supervisor: Who is the contact listed against the request? I need a specific route for the status check.
  5. Ground handling liaison: The provider's duty desk is listed. No individual completion report has been received from it.
  6. Turnaround supervisor: I'll contact that desk and record who supplies the update, keeping the request reference attached.
  7. Ground handling liaison: The performance dashboard currently groups acknowledged and completed requests together. Please don't rely on that label.
  8. Turnaround supervisor: I'll flag the dashboard ambiguity separately. It doesn't change the actual open status of this task.
  9. Ground handling liaison: Thank you. Please confirm that the receiving shift owns the next provider contact.
  10. Turnaround supervisor: Confirmed. I own that contact and will communicate verified progress without declaring the turnaround complete.
A document check passes to another shift

Separate fictional ground-handling exercise: two load-sheet document versions are present; an authorized document check is pending. Staff discuss ownership, not loading instructions.

  1. Document coordinator: Two load-sheet versions are attached to the coordination record, and the authorized version check remains pending.
  2. Ground operations lead: I'm taking over the coordination desk. Has either version been identified as current by the responsible team?
  3. Document coordinator: Not in the messages we have. I requested clarification but haven't received their response.
  4. Ground operations lead: I'll accept follow-up with that team. Please leave both references visible so the conflict is clear.
  5. Document coordinator: Would you prefer me to delete the older-looking attachment before the handoff to reduce confusion?
  6. Ground operations lead: No. Appearance doesn't establish status, and we need the records preserved under the applicable process.
  7. Document coordinator: Understood. I'll mark the unresolved version question clearly, without making a loading or release decision.
  8. Ground operations lead: I'll obtain the authorized status and confirm it to the receiving coordination team.
  9. Document coordinator: So the open task is document clarification, and you own the next contact with the responsible team?
  10. Ground operations lead: Exactly. Our handoff transfers follow-up responsibility, not authority to resolve the technical discrepancy ourselves.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 05: Ground Handling and Turnaround Performance
Original fictional case: At 13:20 UTC, a fictional turnaround requires 96 catering packs. The receiving record confirms 80 packs, leaving 16 outstanding. A supplier truck has arrived with a note claiming the remaining 16, but receipt and count are not yet verified. The outgoing ground team sent a handover without obtaining a named receiving person's acceptance. Coordinator Hassan contacts incoming lead Elin. She can accept responsibility for verifying the balance and reporting status at 13:35 UTC; acceptance does not itself complete the delivery. The 13:45 UTC off-block target remains a planning target. Other required turnaround and release processes are outside this short coordination case.
Language workshop: A handover was sent, but nobody accepted it
Communication goal: Confirm task ownership and report a catering balance without confusing arrival, receipt, and completion.
Language guidance: 96 required minus 80 confirmed received leaves 16 outstanding. The supplier note claims those 16 are on the truck; it does not prove they have been counted and accepted. Keep the receiving balance unchanged until verification.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We have 80 confirmed against a requirement of 96. / Sixteen packs remain outstanding. / The truck is here, but receipt is not verified. / The delivery note lists the remaining sixteen. / The earlier message has no named acceptance. / Can you accept the balance-verification task? / I will verify receipt and count, then report the result. / Please provide status at 13:35 UTC. / That is a reporting time, not a guarantee of completion. / 13:45 UTC remains the off-block target. / Catering status is not the whole turnaround status. / Elin has accepted responsibility for the check. / Any unverified balance remains open. / We have not established the cause of the handover gap. / Ninety-six required, eighty received, sixteen pending verification. / Change the receiving balance only after verified receipt.
Vocabulary: turnaround: The ground-service period between an aircraft's arrival and next departure. / ground handler: A person or organization providing aircraft ground services. / service handover: Transfer of a service task and its current information. / receiving lead: The person accepting responsibility at the receiving end. / catering uplift: Food and service supplies provided for a flight. / catering pack: A counted package of catering supplies in the stated case. / required quantity: The amount specified as needed. / confirmed receipt: Recorded verification that specified goods were received. / outstanding balance: The amount still needed after verified receipt. / delivery note: A supplier document describing a delivery. / manifest: A list of the goods or people covered by an operation. / truck arrival: The event of the delivery vehicle reaching the site. / physical count: A check of the actual quantity present or received. / short delivery: Receipt of less than the required quantity. / task acceptance: Explicit agreement to take responsibility for an activity. / task completion: Verified finishing of the specified activity. / milestone: A defined event used to track progress. / critical path: The sequence of dependent activities determining the earliest completion. / service dependency: A required service relationship affecting later work. / target off-block time: A planning target for leaving the parking position. / readiness status: The verified position of preparation for the next step. / handover gap: Missing information or acceptance during a transfer. / exception report: A message identifying a departure from the planned condition. / delay attribution: Assigning a cause or category to a delay using the applicable evidence.
Speaking task: Read the corrected dialogue, keeping 96 required, 80 received, and 16 outstanding distinct. Switch roles and repeat turns 9-16; separate acceptance of responsibility, the 13:35 report, and catering completion. Read the checked transfer twice using 115 received, five outstanding, Jo, and 14:10 UTC.
Partner: Hassan: Turnaround coordinator / Elin: Incoming ground-services lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A service requires 120 packs. The record confirms 100, then verifies 15 more. Lead Jo accepts responsibility for the remaining check and will report at 14:10 UTC.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Hassan: We should review why the earlier handover had no acceptance, using the messages instead of immediately blaming a department.
Elin: Yes. The handover gap is established, but its cause is not. Preserve the messages and review the process. Solving the current ownership problem does not settle the later causal question.
Hassan: Let me read back the current arrangement: ninety-six required, eighty confirmed received, sixteen pending verification, Elin responsible, and a status report at thirteen thirty-five UTC.
Elin: That captures my task acceptance. Keep thirteen forty-five labeled as the off-block target. I will report the verified catering balance and any remaining exception at the agreed check-in.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Security and Access ControlA meeting invitation is not access authorization+

Your goal

Refuse an access shortcut professionally and offer the approved verification route without implying automatic entry.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

A meeting invitation is not access authorization

Visitor Mr Vale arrives late for a meeting in a restricted airport work area. He shows an email invitation but has not completed the fictional site's visitor verification process. He asks employee Anika to admit him using her access and says the host will approve later. Anika is not authorized to bypass verification or provide an unapproved escort. She can keep him at public reception, contact the host through the approved directory, and refer the request to the access desk. Any permitted entry must follow the site's actual authorization and escort requirements. No entry permission is established in this case.

Anika: Airport employee / Mr Vale: Visitor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the email invitation establish in this case?

Answer and explanation

B. A claimed meeting arrangement, not completed access authorization

The visitor still has to complete the site's separate verification and authorization process.

2. What can Anika do now?

Answer and explanation

C. Contact the host through the approved directory and refer to the access desk.

The brief permits the official verification route but not a bypass or unapproved escort.

3. Where should the visitor remain under the stated process?

Answer and explanation

C. Public reception

The case explicitly keeps the visitor at public reception while the request is handled.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A meeting invitation and host familiarity do not replace the supplied access process. Anika's own credential does not establish permission for the visitor, and her employee status does not establish that she may escort him.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
restricted area
A location where entry requires specified authorization.protect a restricted area
access control
Measures determining who may enter or use a location or resource.follow access control
access authorization
Permission for a person to enter the specified area.verify access authorization
visitor verification
The approved process for checking a visitor's identity and purpose.complete visitor verification
identity check
Confirmation that a person is who they claim to be.conduct an identity check
visitor badge
A credential issued for an authorized visitor under local rules.verify a visitor badge
access credential
A token or document used to demonstrate permitted access.protect an access credential
credential sharing
Allowing another person to use an assigned access credential.prohibit credential sharing
escort authorization
Permission to accompany a visitor under the site's rules.verify escort authorization
approved escort
A person permitted to accompany a visitor under the relevant conditions.arrange an approved escort
host confirmation
Verification of the visit through the responsible host.request host confirmation
approved directory
A trusted source of official contact information.use the approved directory
public reception
A visitor-contact location outside the restricted area.wait at public reception
access desk
The function handling access requests and verification.contact the access desk
visitor register
The record of visits under the local process.update the visitor register
access scope
The areas and conditions covered by a permission.confirm access scope
validity period
The time during which a credential or permission applies.check the validity period
expired credential
A credential whose permitted period has ended.identify an expired credential
access exception
A departure from the normal rule requiring the relevant authority.verify an access exception
challenge procedure
The approved response for checking questionable access.follow the challenge procedure
security escalation
Referral of an access concern through the security process.use security escalation
urgency claim
A statement that time pressure requires immediate action.assess an urgency claim
authorization boundary
The limit of what a person or permission allows.maintain the authorization boundary
unverified visitor
A person whose required visitor checks are incomplete.refer an unverified visitor

Host confirmation, identity verification, permission scope, and any escort conditions are distinct checks within the actual site process. This language case provides no shortcut, complete security procedure, or guarantee that entry will be approved.

C Notice the language

A firm boundary can include practical help

Urgency, familiarity, and seniority may make a shortcut sound socially reasonable. A professional refusal can acknowledge the late meeting while preserving the access rule. Offer the approved route without accusing the visitor of criminal intent or promising that verification will succeed.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge urgency
I understand that your meeting is running late.
State the boundary
I cannot admit you before the required verification.
Separate invitation and access
The invitation does not complete the access process.
Protect personal access
I cannot let you use my credential.
Avoid an unapproved escort
I am not authorized to bypass the escort requirements.
Offer a practical route
I can contact the host through our approved directory.
Name the responsible desk
The access desk will handle the verification request.
Give the current location
Please remain at public reception while we contact them.
Avoid accusing intent
This is an authorization requirement, not a judgment about you.
Keep the outcome open
I cannot promise entry before the checks are complete.
Reject retrospective approval
Approval afterward would not satisfy the current entry requirement.
Check the relevant scope
Any permission must cover the required area.
Respect the actual process
We will follow the site's authorization and escort rules.
Keep the host informed
I can tell the host that you are waiting here.
Do not self-authorize
I cannot grant an exception on my own.
Close helpfully
Let us use the approved route to resolve the request.
6 language and precision notes
Invited versus authorized
A meeting arrangement does not settle restricted-area entry.
My credential
Belongs to its assigned user and does not transfer the user's rights.
Afterward
Too late to satisfy a prior-entry condition in this case.
Can contact
Offers a step without guaranteeing approval.
Not authorized
States a role boundary without blaming the visitor.
Approved route
Refers to the actual site's process, not an improvised workaround.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response is both firm and helpful?

Answer and explanation

A. I cannot admit you yet; I can contact your host and the access desk from reception.

The correct response preserves the boundary and offers the permitted practical next step.

2. Which statement is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. Anika may escort anyone because she works here.

Employee status does not establish escort authority under the supplied site process.

3. What should Anika use to contact the host?

Answer and explanation

B. The approved directory

The case identifies the approved directory as the trusted contact route.

4. What does a pending access request mean?

Answer and explanation

C. Entry is not yet established as authorized.

Pending authorization is a current process status, not a verdict on intent or a permanent refusal.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

A meeting invitation is not access authorization

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Anika: Airport employee / Mr Vale: Visitor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • escort authorization
  • credential sharing
  • access authorization
  • authorization boundary
  • access exception
  • visitor verification
  • access desk
  • access scope
  • public reception
  • approved directory
  1. Mr Vale
    I am late for a meeting inside. Here is the email invitation. Could you let me through with you? The host can confirm everything after I arrive.
  2. Anika
    I understand the time pressure, but 1 has not been established. I cannot admit you before the required verification. I can help contact your host from public reception.
  3. Mr Vale
    I have visited before, and the host knows me well. Doesn't that count for something?
  4. Anika
    We still need 2 for this visit. The invitation explains the meeting request, but it does not complete the entry process. I will use the approved route to check it.
  5. Mr Vale
    Could I borrow your access? I will return it immediately. You can explain that you were helping me reach the meeting.
  6. Anika
    I cannot permit 3. My access applies to me, not automatically to another person. Please remain here while I contact the host and the access desk.
  7. Mr Vale
    Then perhaps you can escort me. You are an employee, and I would stay beside you all the way to the room.
  8. Anika
    Employee status does not establish 4. I cannot bypass the site's escort requirements. If an escort is appropriate, the access desk must arrange it through the actual process.
  9. Mr Vale
    I can give you the host's mobile number. It will be faster than searching, and they will certainly tell you to let me in.
  10. Anika
    I will contact the host through the 5. That is our trusted route. Host confirmation is useful, but any entry must also meet the site's authorization and escort requirements.
  11. Mr Vale
    All right. Please make clear that I arrived for a real meeting. I do not want the host to think I simply failed to turn up.
  12. Anika
    I can tell them you are waiting at 6. That gives the host an accurate update without implying that you have already been admitted or that the access checks are complete.
  13. Mr Vale
    Are you refusing to help, or is there an unfinished step? The meeting may finish before we resolve this.
  14. Anika
    I can help, but I have an 7: I cannot grant entry or an escort exception. I can contact the host and ask about meeting here while verification is pending.
  15. Mr Vale
    Could the host approve it afterward if everyone agrees that the delay was unreasonable? That would avoid losing more of the meeting now.
  16. Anika
    A later 8 cannot be assumed as permission to bypass the current requirement. Let us ask the access desk what approved arrangements are possible before anyone crosses the boundary.
  17. Mr Vale
    Understood. I will wait here. Please ask whether the host can meet me in reception or speak with me while the access request is being handled.
  18. Anika
    I can pass on that request. The 9 will handle the verification, and the host can respond about meeting arrangements. Neither response should be treated as complete until confirmed.
  19. Mr Vale
    Please tell the host I am at reception and still want to meet. I can wait here while the access desk checks the request.
  20. Anika
    I will. Any approved entry must have the right 10 and satisfy the actual site requirements. For now, you remain here and I will contact the responsible people through the approved route.

Answers and explanations
  1. access authorization Turn 2. Access authorization must be established through the site's process before entry; an invitation and urgency do not provide it.
  2. visitor verification Turn 4. Visitor verification is the current required process, which prior visits and familiarity do not replace.
  3. credential sharing Turn 6. Credential sharing would let another person use Anika's assigned access, which the supplied process does not authorize.
  4. escort authorization Turn 8. Escort authorization is a separate permission; Anika is not authorized to provide an unapproved escort simply because she works there.
  5. approved directory Turn 10. The approved directory is the trusted contact source specified in the case, rather than relying solely on details from an unverified visitor.
  6. public reception Turn 12. Public reception is the permitted waiting location while the access request is verified.
  7. authorization boundary Turn 14. The authorization boundary limits Anika's permission to admit or escort a visitor, not her ability to contact the responsible desk.
  8. access exception Turn 16. An access exception requires the relevant authority and cannot be assumed retroactively to justify bypassing the current entry requirement.
  9. access desk Turn 18. The access desk is the responsible function handling verification and possible permitted arrangements, not a guaranteed approval service.
  10. access scope Turn 20. Access scope identifies the specific areas and conditions covered by permission, rather than treating any approval as unrestricted entry.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mr Vale: I am late for a meeting inside. Here is the email invitation. Could you let me through with you? The host can confirm everything after I arrive.
  2. Anika: I understand the time pressure, but access authorization has not been established. I cannot admit you before the required verification. I can help contact your host from public reception.
  3. Mr Vale: I have visited before, and the host knows me well. Doesn't that count for something?
  4. Anika: We still need visitor verification for this visit. The invitation explains the meeting request, but it does not complete the entry process. I will use the approved route to check it.
  5. Mr Vale: Could I borrow your access? I will return it immediately. You can explain that you were helping me reach the meeting.
  6. Anika: I cannot permit credential sharing. My access applies to me, not automatically to another person. Please remain here while I contact the host and the access desk.
  7. Mr Vale: Then perhaps you can escort me. You are an employee, and I would stay beside you all the way to the room.
  8. Anika: Employee status does not establish escort authorization. I cannot bypass the site's escort requirements. If an escort is appropriate, the access desk must arrange it through the actual process.
  9. Mr Vale: I can give you the host's mobile number. It will be faster than searching, and they will certainly tell you to let me in.
  10. Anika: I will contact the host through the approved directory. That is our trusted route. Host confirmation is useful, but any entry must also meet the site's authorization and escort requirements.
  11. Mr Vale: All right. Please make clear that I arrived for a real meeting. I do not want the host to think I simply failed to turn up.
  12. Anika: I can tell them you are waiting at public reception. That gives the host an accurate update without implying that you have already been admitted or that the access checks are complete.
  13. Mr Vale: Are you refusing to help, or is there an unfinished step? The meeting may finish before we resolve this.
  14. Anika: I can help, but I have an authorization boundary: I cannot grant entry or an escort exception. I can contact the host and ask about meeting here while verification is pending.
  15. Mr Vale: Could the host approve it afterward if everyone agrees that the delay was unreasonable? That would avoid losing more of the meeting now.
  16. Anika: A later access exception cannot be assumed as permission to bypass the current requirement. Let us ask the access desk what approved arrangements are possible before anyone crosses the boundary.
  17. Mr Vale: Understood. I will wait here. Please ask whether the host can meet me in reception or speak with me while the access request is being handled.
  18. Anika: I can pass on that request. The access desk will handle the verification, and the host can respond about meeting arrangements. Neither response should be treated as complete until confirmed.
  19. Mr Vale: Please tell the host I am at reception and still want to meet. I can wait here while the access desk checks the request.
  20. Anika: I will. Any approved entry must have the right access scope and satisfy the actual site requirements. For now, you remain here and I will contact the responsible people through the approved route.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A late meeting does not bypass access checks

Fictional landside reception exercise: a contractor asks to bypass a secure-area access check because a meeting is late; no access procedure details are taught.

  1. Contractor representative: Our meeting has started. Could you let us through now and check the paperwork afterward?
  2. Access coordinator: Please remain at reception while we complete the applicable access process. I can't bypass that check.
  3. Contractor representative: Our host knows us and said an escort would be available when we arrived.
  4. Access coordinator: I'll contact the host to confirm the escort arrangements, but that doesn't replace the required checks.
  5. Contractor representative: What information do you need from me so this request reaches the correct person?
  6. Access coordinator: Please provide the meeting host's name through this reception process, and I'll request their confirmation.
  7. Contractor representative: Can you promise we'll enter the secure area within five minutes if the host answers?
  8. Access coordinator: I can't promise completion time. I'll explain the status and ask the host to adjust the meeting.
  9. Contractor representative: Understood. I'll wait here and tell my colleagues not to follow another group through.
  10. Access coordinator: Thank you. We'll use the established badge and escort process without making an exception for lateness.
An unreadable badge needs the proper check

Separate fictional reception case: a visiting technician's badge cannot be read by reception; access has not been authorized.

  1. Visiting technician: My badge isn't reading. Can you use my colleague's badge to get us both inside?
  2. Security receptionist: No. Please stay at reception while I contact the authorized access team about your badge.
  3. Visiting technician: I'm due at an equipment meeting. What should I tell the manager waiting for me?
  4. Security receptionist: Tell them the access check is unresolved. I can request that they contact reception about the delay.
  5. Visiting technician: Could you confirm whether the problem means my permission has been withdrawn by security?
  6. Security receptionist: We haven't established the reason. An unreadable badge alone doesn't tell me your authorization status.
  7. Visiting technician: Then please ask the access team to check the status rather than guessing the cause.
  8. Security receptionist: I will. I'll also keep any identifying details within the approved reception and access channels.
  9. Visiting technician: I'll wait for the verified result and won't borrow another person's badge for entry.
  10. Security receptionist: Thank you. The immediate request is a proper status check, not an alternative route into the secure area.
Request the access audit evidence securely

Separate fictional administrative case: an internal audit needs access-check records for a defined training sample; no personal records are shared in the dialogue.

  1. Access audit coordinator: Could you provide the approved access-check records for the training sample through our restricted review channel?
  2. Reception supervisor: Do you need every visitor record, or only those listed in the audit request?
  3. Access audit coordinator: Only the listed sample. We need evidence of the checks without collecting unrelated personal details.
  4. Reception supervisor: The request mentions escort confirmation too. Should I include those linked records where they exist?
  5. Access audit coordinator: Yes, for the same sample, and clearly identify missing evidence rather than reconstructing an event.
  6. Reception supervisor: Can I send the bundle to the whole station mailing list so nobody misses it?
  7. Access audit coordinator: Please don't. Use the authorized review channel and recipient list specified for this exercise.
  8. Reception supervisor: I'll check which records are available and tell you if retrieval needs more time.
  9. Access audit coordinator: That works. Please confirm an achievable delivery point after checking, not an assumed deadline.
  10. Reception supervisor: I'll respond with the available sample records, any gaps, and the timing for outstanding retrieval.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A meeting invitation is not access authorization
  1. Read turns 1-10; acknowledge the late meeting before stating the access boundary.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep reception and the approved contact route explicit without accusing the visitor.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep invitation and permission separate

A visitor has a meeting invitation but incomplete access verification. Employee Jo cannot share credentials or approve an escort. Jo can contact the host through the approved directory and refer to the access desk.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Visitor: "My invitation does not itself establish access ___."

Answer and explanation

C. authorization

The invitation and the required entry permission are separate in this case.

2. Employee: "I cannot share my ___."

Answer and explanation

D. credential

Jo's assigned access cannot be used as permission for the visitor.

3. Visitor: "Please contact the host through the approved ___."

Answer and explanation

C. directory

The supplied trusted contact route is the approved directory.

4. Employee: "The access ___ will handle the verification request."

Answer and explanation

C. desk

The case assigns the verification request to the access desk.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 06: Security and Access Control
Original fictional case: Visitor Mr Vale arrives late for a meeting in a restricted airport work area. He shows an email invitation but has not completed the fictional site's visitor verification process. He asks employee Anika to admit him using her access and says the host will approve later. Anika is not authorized to bypass verification or provide an unapproved escort. She can keep him at public reception, contact the host through the approved directory, and refer the request to the access desk. Any permitted entry must follow the site's actual authorization and escort requirements. No entry permission is established in this case.
Language workshop: A meeting invitation is not access authorization
Communication goal: Refuse an access shortcut professionally and offer the approved verification route without implying automatic entry.
Language guidance: A meeting invitation and host familiarity do not replace the supplied access process. Anika's own credential does not establish permission for the visitor, and her employee status does not establish that she may escort him.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand that your meeting is running late. / I cannot admit you before the required verification. / The invitation does not complete the access process. / I cannot let you use my credential. / I am not authorized to bypass the escort requirements. / I can contact the host through our approved directory. / The access desk will handle the verification request. / Please remain at public reception while we contact them. / This is an authorization requirement, not a judgment about you. / I cannot promise entry before the checks are complete. / Approval afterward would not satisfy the current entry requirement. / Any permission must cover the required area. / We will follow the site's authorization and escort rules. / I can tell the host that you are waiting here. / I cannot grant an exception on my own. / Let us use the approved route to resolve the request.
Vocabulary: restricted area: A location where entry requires specified authorization. / access control: Measures determining who may enter or use a location or resource. / access authorization: Permission for a person to enter the specified area. / visitor verification: The approved process for checking a visitor's identity and purpose. / identity check: Confirmation that a person is who they claim to be. / visitor badge: A credential issued for an authorized visitor under local rules. / access credential: A token or document used to demonstrate permitted access. / credential sharing: Allowing another person to use an assigned access credential. / escort authorization: Permission to accompany a visitor under the site's rules. / approved escort: A person permitted to accompany a visitor under the relevant conditions. / host confirmation: Verification of the visit through the responsible host. / approved directory: A trusted source of official contact information. / public reception: A visitor-contact location outside the restricted area. / access desk: The function handling access requests and verification. / visitor register: The record of visits under the local process. / access scope: The areas and conditions covered by a permission. / validity period: The time during which a credential or permission applies. / expired credential: A credential whose permitted period has ended. / access exception: A departure from the normal rule requiring the relevant authority. / challenge procedure: The approved response for checking questionable access. / security escalation: Referral of an access concern through the security process. / urgency claim: A statement that time pressure requires immediate action. / authorization boundary: The limit of what a person or permission allows. / unverified visitor: A person whose required visitor checks are incomplete.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; acknowledge the late meeting before stating the access boundary. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep reception and the approved contact route explicit without accusing the visitor. Read the checked transfer twice, distinguishing an invitation, authorization, a credential, and the responsible desk.
Partner: Anika: Airport employee / Mr Vale: Visitor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A visitor has a meeting invitation but incomplete access verification. Employee Jo cannot share credentials or approve an escort. Jo can contact the host through the approved directory and refer to the access desk.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mr Vale: Understood. I will wait here. Please ask whether the host can meet me in reception or speak with me while the access request is being handled.
Anika: I can pass on that request. The access desk will handle the verification, and the host can respond about meeting arrangements. Neither response should be treated as complete until confirmed.
Mr Vale: Please tell the host I am at reception and still want to meet. I can wait here while the access desk checks the request.
Anika: I will. Any approved entry must have the right access scope and satisfy the actual site requirements. For now, you remain here and I will contact the responsible people through the approved route.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Incident Reporting and InvestigationAn observed dent became an unsupported cause+

Your goal

Separate firsthand observation, another person's report, and a causal conclusion in an occurrence record.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

An observed dent became an unsupported cause

Ground-services employee Rosa observed a dent in the side panel of baggage cart C4 at 09:42 local time. She did not see an impact. Colleague Ivo reports hearing a bang near the cart at approximately 09:39, but did not see contact either. Relevant camera footage has not been reviewed. A draft report says that a tug driver struck C4 and caused the damage; no supplied evidence establishes that claim. Safety reviewer Daniel helps Rosa correct the draft while preserving the original version and the source of each account. Immediate operational controls follow the separate local process; this exchange does not authorize equipment return to use.

Rosa: Ground-services employee / Daniel: Safety reviewer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What did Rosa directly observe?

Answer and explanation

A. A dent in cart C4 at 09:42 local

Rosa saw the damage at 09:42 but did not witness the contact or its cause.

2. What is Ivo's account?

Answer and explanation

B. He heard a bang at approximately 09:39 without seeing contact.

The brief attributes a reported sound and approximate time to Ivo, not a witnessed impact.

3. Which claim should be removed as unsupported?

Answer and explanation

A. A tug driver struck C4 and caused the damage.

The supplied observations and reported sound do not establish a particular actor or causal contact.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

09:42 is the time Rosa observed the dent, not a verified impact time. Ivo's approximately 09:39 sound is a separate reported observation. A three-minute sequence does not identify the contact, actor, or cause.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
occurrence report
A record describing an event or condition relevant to safety.submit an occurrence report
initial report
The first recorded account before the review is complete.qualify an initial report
firsthand account
Information from a person's own observation.preserve a firsthand account
witness statement
An attributed account from someone with relevant observations.record a witness statement
reported sound
An auditory event described by a person.attribute a reported sound
observation time
When a person noticed a condition.state the observation time
event time
When the event itself occurred.distinguish the event time
approximate time
A time given with limited precision.label an approximate time
chronology
The sequence of relevant events or observations.establish the chronology
causal claim
A statement that one event produced another.test a causal claim
contributing factor
A condition that helped produce or shape an event.assess contributing factors
root cause
An underlying cause identified through the relevant analysis.avoid premature root-cause claims
evidence source
The origin of information used in a review.identify the evidence source
corroboration
Independent support for an account or inference.seek corroboration
camera footage
Recorded video that may provide relevant evidence.preserve camera footage
review status
The current stage of an examination of evidence.state the review status
damage description
Factual wording identifying the observed physical condition.give a damage description
equipment identifier
The code distinguishing the equipment involved.verify the equipment identifier
baggage cart
A vehicle or trailer used to carry baggage on the ground.identify the baggage cart
tug
A vehicle used to move other ground equipment or aircraft under the relevant operation.identify the reported tug
attribution
Stating who supplied a claim or observation.preserve source attribution
version history
The record of changes to a document over time.retain version history
factual amendment
A documented correction or addition to the factual record.make a factual amendment
causation finding
A supported conclusion about what produced the event.distinguish a causation finding

Footage not yet reviewed must not be described as confirming or disproving the draft. Correct the unsupported claim transparently, preserve the original version, and keep sources separate. The report is not equipment-release authorization.

C Notice the language

Neutral does not mean vague

A report can be specific about times, locations, equipment, and observations without assigning premature blame. Avoiding an unsupported causal claim protects the investigation; it does not require deleting useful facts or minimizing the seriousness of reported damage.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Start with firsthand information
I observed a dent on the side panel of cart C4.
State the observation time
I noticed it at 09:42 local time.
Avoid claiming an impact
I did not see contact occur.
Attribute the second account
Ivo reports hearing a bang near the cart.
Preserve time uncertainty
He gives the time as approximately 09:39.
Separate observation and event
That is when I saw the damage, not necessarily when it occurred.
Describe the evidence gap
The camera footage has not been reviewed.
Remove the unsupported cause
The supplied evidence does not identify a tug strike.
Keep the equipment precise
The affected cart identifier is C4.
Preserve the original
Retain the earlier draft in the version history.
Record the correction
Add a dated factual amendment explaining the change.
Avoid personal judgment
We should report actions and evidence, not assumed motives.
Leave investigation open
The cause remains under review.
Avoid false exoneration
Removing the accusation does not establish that no contact occurred.
Keep operational authority separate
This report does not clear the equipment for use.
Close the account
Distinguish what I saw, what I was told, and what remains unknown.
6 language and precision notes
Observed at
Gives the time of noticing, not necessarily the event time.
Reports hearing
Attributes the account and preserves that it is auditory.
Approximately
Retains limited precision; do not silently turn it into an exact timestamp.
Not reviewed
Says nothing about what the footage will show.
Unsupported
Not established by the current evidence, not necessarily disproved.
Amendment
Correct transparently rather than erase the history.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which sentence is supported?

Answer and explanation

D. Rosa observed a dent on C4 at 09:42 local and did not witness contact.

The correct sentence limits the account to Rosa's direct observation and its known boundary.

2. How should Ivo's time appear?

Answer and explanation

A. Approximately 09:39, attributed to Ivo

The source supplied an approximate sound time, which should retain both attribution and uncertainty.

3. What should happen to the original draft?

Answer and explanation

B. Preserve it and document the correction in version history.

Transparent amendment preserves the record without retaining the unsupported causal assertion as fact.

4. What does removing the causal accusation establish?

Answer and explanation

D. The record no longer presents an unverified cause as proved.

A more accurate record does not itself determine causation, exoneration, or operational release.

E Conversations

An observed dent became an unsupported cause

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rosa: Ground-services employee / Daniel: Safety reviewer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • causation finding
  • approximate time
  • observation time
  • causal claim
  • camera footage
  • version history
  • damage description
  • attribution
  • factual amendment
  • firsthand account
  1. Daniel
    The draft says a tug driver struck cart C4 and caused the damage. Before we keep that sentence, what did you personally observe?
  2. Rosa
    My 1 is narrower. I saw a dent on C4's side panel at nine forty-two local time. I did not see any impact or contact.
  3. Daniel
    Then nine forty-two is the time you noticed the damage. We should not automatically label it the time the damaging event occurred.
  4. Rosa
    Correct. It is the 2, not a verified event time. I do not know how long the dent had been there when I saw it.
  5. Daniel
    The draft also mentions a bang at nine thirty-nine. Did you hear that yourself, or is that information from somebody else's account?
  6. Rosa
    That needs 3. Ivo told me he heard a bang near the cart at approximately nine thirty-nine. He did not see contact either, so his account does not identify an impact.
  7. Daniel
    Keep approximately. Turning a remembered time into an exact timestamp would give readers more precision than the source actually provides.
  8. Rosa
    I will preserve the 4 and name the source. The revised chronology can show his reported sound and my later observation without treating them as a proven causal sequence.
  9. Daniel
    That sequence may be relevant, but it does not identify who caused the dent. Is there any reviewed video or other evidence supporting the tug statement?
  10. Rosa
    The 5 has not been reviewed. I should not describe it as supporting the claim, and I cannot say it rules the claim out either.
  11. Daniel
    Exactly. The investigation can examine relevant evidence through the approved process. The initial report should not announce what that later review will conclude.
  12. Rosa
    I will remove the unsupported 6 from the factual account. The report will say what was observed and reported, with the cause still under review.
  13. Daniel
    Removing the accusation should not erase the fact that damage was observed. Neutral reporting can still be specific about the equipment and its physical condition.
  14. Rosa
    I will retain the 7, the C4 identifier, and the observation time. I will avoid substituting a vague phrase such as something happened somewhere on the ramp.
  15. Daniel
    Has anyone already received that draft? We need the correction to reach those recipients and remain linked to the original version.
  16. Rosa
    I will preserve the 8 and add a dated amendment explaining the correction. That keeps the original wording traceable while making the current factual account accurate.
  17. Daniel
    Good. Also keep this reporting conversation separate from the equipment's operational status. A revised report is not permission to put the cart back into use.
  18. Rosa
    Understood. Any 9 changes the account, not the separate local controls. Those controls continue under the relevant process, and this exchange does not authorize return to use.
  19. Daniel
    Before we send the amendment, please read back your observation, Ivo's account, and the point the investigation has not established.
  20. Rosa
    I saw the dent at nine forty-two. Ivo reports a bang at approximately nine thirty-nine. The footage is unreviewed, and there is no established 10 identifying how the damage occurred.

Answers and explanations
  1. firsthand account Turn 2. A firsthand account covers Rosa's own observation of the dent, not a contact or cause she did not witness.
  2. observation time Turn 4. Observation time records when Rosa noticed the dent; the actual time of the damage-producing event remains unverified.
  3. attribution Turn 6. Attribution identifies Ivo as the source of the reported sound and prevents it from becoming Rosa's claimed firsthand observation.
  4. approximate time Turn 8. Approximate time marks the limited precision of Ivo's reported auditory observation rather than asserting an exact impact timestamp.
  5. camera footage Turn 10. Camera footage exists as potential evidence in the case, but its contents have not been reviewed or established.
  6. causal claim Turn 12. The causal claim assigns the damage to a tug driver without evidence establishing that contact, actor, or causal link.
  7. damage description Turn 14. The damage description is the observed dent on C4's side panel, which remains relevant even when the unsupported cause is removed.
  8. version history Turn 16. Version history retains the earlier draft and the documented change, rather than silently deleting the unsupported wording.
  9. factual amendment Turn 18. A factual amendment corrects the record; it does not provide operational clearance or replace the site's equipment-control process.
  10. causation finding Turn 20. A causation finding would require a supported conclusion from the appropriate review, which the supplied observations do not yet provide.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Daniel: The draft says a tug driver struck cart C4 and caused the damage. Before we keep that sentence, what did you personally observe?
  2. Rosa: My firsthand account is narrower. I saw a dent on C4's side panel at nine forty-two local time. I did not see any impact or contact.
  3. Daniel: Then nine forty-two is the time you noticed the damage. We should not automatically label it the time the damaging event occurred.
  4. Rosa: Correct. It is the observation time, not a verified event time. I do not know how long the dent had been there when I saw it.
  5. Daniel: The draft also mentions a bang at nine thirty-nine. Did you hear that yourself, or is that information from somebody else's account?
  6. Rosa: That needs attribution. Ivo told me he heard a bang near the cart at approximately nine thirty-nine. He did not see contact either, so his account does not identify an impact.
  7. Daniel: Keep approximately. Turning a remembered time into an exact timestamp would give readers more precision than the source actually provides.
  8. Rosa: I will preserve the approximate time and name the source. The revised chronology can show his reported sound and my later observation without treating them as a proven causal sequence.
  9. Daniel: That sequence may be relevant, but it does not identify who caused the dent. Is there any reviewed video or other evidence supporting the tug statement?
  10. Rosa: The camera footage has not been reviewed. I should not describe it as supporting the claim, and I cannot say it rules the claim out either.
  11. Daniel: Exactly. The investigation can examine relevant evidence through the approved process. The initial report should not announce what that later review will conclude.
  12. Rosa: I will remove the unsupported causal claim from the factual account. The report will say what was observed and reported, with the cause still under review.
  13. Daniel: Removing the accusation should not erase the fact that damage was observed. Neutral reporting can still be specific about the equipment and its physical condition.
  14. Rosa: I will retain the damage description, the C4 identifier, and the observation time. I will avoid substituting a vague phrase such as something happened somewhere on the ramp.
  15. Daniel: Has anyone already received that draft? We need the correction to reach those recipients and remain linked to the original version.
  16. Rosa: I will preserve the version history and add a dated amendment explaining the correction. That keeps the original wording traceable while making the current factual account accurate.
  17. Daniel: Good. Also keep this reporting conversation separate from the equipment's operational status. A revised report is not permission to put the cart back into use.
  18. Rosa: Understood. Any factual amendment changes the account, not the separate local controls. Those controls continue under the relevant process, and this exchange does not authorize return to use.
  19. Daniel: Before we send the amendment, please read back your observation, Ivo's account, and the point the investigation has not established.

Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A dent does not establish its cause

Fictional incident-review exercise: equipment damage is observed, but the investigation has not established the action that caused it.

  1. Incident coordinator: The draft says the cart operator caused the dent, but the review is still incomplete.
  2. Ground services manager: The dent was noticed after the cart visit. I thought that sequence supported the wording.
  3. Incident coordinator: It supports a timeline observation, not a confirmed root cause or attribution to one person.
  4. Ground services manager: Then should the incident report say damage was observed after the visit, with cause undetermined?
  5. Incident coordinator: Yes, provided that sequence is supported by the records. Keep the damage assessment separate.
  6. Ground services manager: Can we still propose a corrective action before the investigation establishes exactly what happened?
  7. Incident coordinator: We can refer concerns through the authorized process, but shouldn't call a proposal proof of cause.
  8. Ground services manager: I'll remove the accusation and identify which timestamps and observations actually support the initial account.
  9. Incident coordinator: Good. Preserve the evidence and label any witness interpretation as an account, not an established finding.
  10. Ground services manager: The revised report will distinguish observed damage, recorded sequence, and questions still awaiting investigation.
Two witness accounts are not one finding

Separate fictional investigation case: two witnesses describe a vehicle's position differently; neither account has been reconciled with available records.

  1. Investigation assistant: One witness says the vehicle was stationary, while another says it was moving during the event.
  2. Safety reviewer: Have those accounts been checked against timestamps or other evidence in the incident report?
  3. Investigation assistant: Not yet. I was going to choose the account from the more senior colleague.
  4. Safety reviewer: Seniority doesn't establish accuracy. Record each account and identify the unresolved difference for review.
  5. Investigation assistant: Should I combine them into a neutral sentence saying the vehicle probably moved briefly?
  6. Safety reviewer: That invents a sequence neither account establishes. Keep the disagreement visible instead of smoothing it away.
  7. Investigation assistant: I'll attribute each observation and ask the review team what evidence can clarify the position.
  8. Safety reviewer: Good. The damage assessment may contribute information, but don't assume it will resolve every disagreement.
  9. Investigation assistant: Then our update says the accounts differ and root cause remains under investigation.
  10. Safety reviewer: Exactly. A careful uncertainty statement is more useful than a tidy but unsupported conclusion.
No further reports is not proof of effectiveness

Separate fictional follow-up case: a corrective briefing was delivered last week and no similar incident reports have arrived; effectiveness has not been evaluated.

  1. Safety program manager: No similar reports arrived this week, so our corrective action has eliminated the problem.
  2. Investigation lead: We know the briefing was delivered and no new reports arrived. Elimination is a stronger claim.
  3. Safety program manager: What else could explain the quiet period if the corrective briefing didn't solve it?
  4. Investigation lead: We haven't checked activity levels or reporting completeness, so the comparison may be limited.
  5. Safety program manager: Then I should say the action is implemented, but its effectiveness hasn't been established.
  6. Investigation lead: Yes. Also keep the original root-cause findings distinct from our evaluation of the response.
  7. Safety program manager: Could we ask the safety management team to agree what evidence would show improvement?
  8. Investigation lead: That is a useful next step. They can define the review period and relevant measures.
  9. Safety program manager: I'll revise the update and avoid treating the absence of reports as conclusive evidence.
  10. Investigation lead: Good. The report can recognize completed work while remaining honest about what we still need to assess.

Keep observation time separate from event time

Answer and explanation

B. 10:15

The worker noticed the damage at ten fifteen, not necessarily when it occurred.

2. Reviewer: "The earlier sound is attributed to ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Pat

Pat supplied the auditory account and its approximate time.

3. Worker: "The video remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unreviewed

The case states that its contents have not yet been examined.

4. Reviewer: "No ___ has been established."

Answer and explanation

B. cause

The observations do not identify how or by whom the damage occurred.

Lesson 07: Incident Reporting and Investigation
Original fictional case: Ground-services employee Rosa observed a dent in the side panel of baggage cart C4 at 09:42 local time. She did not see an impact. Colleague Ivo reports hearing a bang near the cart at approximately 09:39, but did not see contact either. Relevant camera footage has not been reviewed. A draft report says that a tug driver struck C4 and caused the damage; no supplied evidence establishes that claim. Immediate operational controls follow the separate local process; this exchange does not authorize equipment return to use.

Communication goal: Separate firsthand observation, another person's report, and a causal conclusion in an occurrence record.
Language guidance: 09:42 is the time Rosa observed the dent, not a verified impact time. Ivo's approximately 09:39 sound is a separate reported observation. A three-minute sequence does not identify the contact, actor, or cause.

Rosa: Understood. Any factual amendment changes the account, not the separate local controls. Those controls continue under the relevant process, and this exchange does not authorize return to use.
Daniel: Before we send the amendment, please read back your observation, Ivo's account, and the point the investigation has not established.
Rosa: I saw the dent at nine forty-two. Ivo reports a bang at approximately nine thirty-nine. The footage is unreviewed, and there is no established causation finding identifying how the damage occurred.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

REFERENCE
Course: Aviation English
Professional audience: aviation operations managers, airline staff, maintenance coordinators, safety teams, dispatchers, airport operations staff, ground handlers, and aviation-adjacent professionals
